Global gradient estimates for the mixed local and nonlocal problems with measurable nonlinearities
Sun-Sig Byun, Deepak Kumar, Ho-Sik Lee

Abstract
. A non-homogeneous mixed local and nonlocal problem in divergence form is investigated for the validity of the global Calderón-Zygmund estimate for the weak solution to the Dirichlet problem of a nonlinear elliptic equation. We establish an optimal Calderón-Zygmund theory by finding not only a minimal regularity requirement on the mixed local and nonlocal operators but also a lower level of geometric assumption on the boundary of the domain for the global gradient estimate. More precisely, assuming that the nonlinearity of the local operator, whose prototype is the classical ( − Δ p ) 1 (-\Delta_{p})^{1} -Laplace operator with 1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty , is measurable in one variable and has a small BMO assumption for the other variables, while the singular kernel associated with the nonlocal ( − Δ q ) s (-\Delta_{q})^{s} -Laplace operator with 0 < s < 1 < q < ∞ 0<s<1<q<\infty is merely measurable, and that the boundary of the domain is sufficiently flat in Reifenberg sense, we prove that the gradient of the solution is as integrable as that of the associated non-homogeneous term in the divergence form.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 35B65; Secondary: 35D30, 35R11, 35R05, 35J60

†
†

S.-S. Byun was supported by NRF-2022R1A2C1009312, D. Kumar was supported by NRF-2021R1A4A1027378 and H.-S. Lee
was supported by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) through (GRK 2235/2
2021 - 282638148) at Bielefeld University.

1.
Introduction and Main Results

In the present paper, we study some regularity properties of weak solutions to the mixed local and nonlocal problem whose prototype is given by

\displaystyle-\textnormal{div}(|Du|^{p-2}Du)+(-\Delta_{q})^{s}u=-\textnormal{div}(|F|^{p-2}F)\quad\text{in}\quad\Omega,

(1.1)

where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
n\geq 2
) is a bounded domain and the operator
(-\Delta_{q})^{s}
is defined by

\displaystyle(-\Delta_{q})^{s}u(x)=P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\dfrac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}\,dy

with constants
s,p
and
q
satisfying

\displaystyle 0<s<1<p,q<\infty

(1.2)

and

\displaystyle p>sq.

(1.3)

Here,
F:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a given vector field such that
|F|\in L^{p}(\Omega)
. The equation (
1.1
) is the Euler-Lagrange equation of the following variational problem:

\displaystyle W^{1,1}(\Omega)\ni u\mapsto\int_{\Omega}[|Du|^{p}-|F|^{p-2}F\cdot Du]\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\dfrac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}\,dx\,dy.

Mixed local and nonlocal problems have been comprehensively studied in recent years due to their extensive applications in the real world. For instance, these kinds of operators model diffusion patterns with different scales and also appears in the theory of
optimal searching strategies, biomathematics, animal foraging and so on; see for instance,
[
32
]
and references therein. Another interesting application comes from the superposition of two stochastic processes with different scales such as a classical random walk and a Lévy process, we refer to
[
33
]
for further details on this. From the mathematical analysis point of view, the mixed local and nonlocal operator can be regarded as the limiting case of the strongly non-homogeneous nonlocal operators,
(-\Delta_{p})^{s_{1}}+(-\Delta_{q})^{s_{2}}
with
0<s_{1},s_{2}<1
, as
s_{1}\to 1
.

1.1
.
State of the Art

The
p
-Laplace problem is a specific example of the following
p
-growth problem:

\displaystyle-\textnormal{div}(A(x,Du))=-\textnormal{div}(|F|^{p-2}F)\quad\text{in }\Omega,

(1.4)

where
F:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
A(\cdot,\cdot):\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a Carathéodory vector field with
|\partial_{z}A(x,z)|\simeq|z|^{p-2}
. In the case of (
1.4
),
|F|\in L^{\gamma}
does not imply
|Du|\in L^{\gamma}
, for every
\gamma>p
, if there is no regularity assumption (with respect to the
x
-variable) on the nonlinearity
A
(cf.
[
53
]
). In this regard, the small BMO assumption is usually considered in the literature, see for instance
[
28
,
47
,
20
,
18
]
, but later the weak small BMO assumption as in Definition
1.1
is recognized as a minimal condition. Moreover, the latter condition allows a big jump in one direction of the variable in
\Omega
, and so our problem is deeply related to the transmission problem, see
[
4
,
35
]
. When
p=2
, the gradient estimate is proved with such a minimal assumption in
[
19
,
34
,
44
]
for the linear case and in
[
13
]
for the linear growth case. In
[
45
]
, a Moser type iteration for the regularized problem of (
1.4
) and an approximation argument are used to consider the
p
-growth case. Recently, the gradient estimate is proved under the setting of generalized
p
-growth problem in
[
14
]
.

The nonlocal nonlinear operators, particularly of the kind
(-\Delta_{q})^{s}
, have been studied extensively in recent years, especially concerning the regularity perspective of solutions involving these operators.
For instance, Di Castro et al.
[
27
]
and Iannizzotto et al.
[
41
]
proved local Hölder continuity results for Dirichlet problems involving the fractional
q
-Laplacian (or the minimizers of corresponding energy functionals). Subsequently, Brasco et al.
[
8
]
obtained higher Hölder continuity results for local weak solutions. Interested readers may refer to
[
10
,
16
,
22
,
39
,
40
,
12
,
26
]
for continuity results of nonlocal problems involving non-standard growth operators. Furthermore, Kuusi et al.
[
48
]
obtained a self-improving property for weak solutions. Recently, Byun and Kim in
[
11
]
, and Diening and Nowak
[
31
]
, independently, obtained Calderón-Zygmund type estimates for nonlocal problems of
p
-growth and irregular kernel coefficients. In this direction, we also mention the works
[
36
,
52
,
55
,
21
]
for some related results on nonlocal problems with certain assumptions on the kernel coefficients.

The linear mixed operators; that is,
p=q=2
in (
1.1
), have a rich literature in terms of the regularity theory, see for instance, the notable works of Dipierro, Valdinoci et al.
[
7
,
6
,
5
]
and references therein. However, the nonlinear case is relatively new and only a few results are known in this case. Particularly, Garain and Kinnunen
[
37
]
established the Hölder continuity results by using the De Giorgi-Nash-Moser technique, for
p=q\neq 2
. Recently, Garain and Lindgren
[
38
]
obtained higher Hölder regularity for weak solutions of (
1.1
) with
p=q\geq 2
; that is, the super-quadratic case, by using the techniques of
[
8
]
. See also
[
54
,
57
,
17
]
and references therein for some other regularity results involving the mixed local-nonlocal type operator appearing as in (
1.1
) with
p=q
. As far as the case
p\neq q
is concerned, we mention the very recent work of De Filippis and Mingione
[
25
]
, where local gradient regularity is established for minimizers of functional whose Euler-Lagrange equation involves the mixed local-nonlocal type operator. Specifically, the gradient of a minimizer
u
of the functional

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}[F(Du)-fu]\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Phi(u(x)-u(y))K(x,y)\,dx\,dy,

where
F(z)\simeq|z|^{p}
,
\Phi(t)\simeq|t|^{q}
and
K(x,y)\simeq|x-y|^{-n-sq}
is a singular kernel, is Hölder continuous if
p>sq
and
f\in L^{\gamma}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\Omega)
with
\gamma>n
. Additionally, they also proved that when the domain
\Omega
is of type
C^{1,\alpha}
, for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
and the exterior data is globally Lipschitz continuous (or more generally it satisfies a certain type of global higher order fractional Sobolev regularity), then the minimizer
u
is globally almost Lipschitz regular. We also refer
[
15
]
for regularity results in case of
p\neq q
for mixed local and nonlocal double phase problems.

1.2
.
Overview of Analytic Tools

In this paper, our aim is to obtain the global behavior of the gradient of weak solutions to mixed local-nonlocal problems, which to the best of our knowledge, was not known earlier. To this end, we consider a more general operator than the one consisting of the sum of the
p
-Laplacian and the
s
-fractional
q
-Laplacian involving a divergence type reaction term, see (
1.7
) with structure conditions (
1.8
) and (
1.9
). Here, we stress that similar to the purely local problems, the nonlinearity of the leading operator is simply a measurable function in one variable and satisfies a small BMO assumption on the rest of the variables. However, for the nonlocal term, the kernel coefficient is merely assumed to be measurable. To prove the desired global Calderón-Zygmund type estimates, the condition on the boundary of the domain also plays an important role. Here, as a minimal condition, we assume that the boundary of the domain is Reifenberg flat set (see Definition
1.4
). Notably, each Lipschitz domain with small Lipschitz constant is Reifenberg flat, but the domain whose boundary satisfies Reifenberg flat condition may not be a Lipschitz domain and such a domain is allowed to have fractal boundaries. For a further study on Reifenberg flat domain, we refer to
[
42
,
49
]
. We point out that in the local elliptic equation, many related articles for global gradient estimates appear as in
[
9
,
18
,
23
,
51
,
56
]
, but in mixed problems there is no such a result under the setting of rough domains even like Lipschitz domains.

In order to achieve the aforementioned gradient estimates, we rely on the maximal function-free technique as developed in
[
1
]
and later used in
[
24
]
. The method heavily depends upon the use of a suitable comparison argument which requires a global self-improving property (small higher integrability of the gradient) of the weak solution to the original problem.
We provide a detailed proof of this in Section 3 as the rigorous analysis done here helps obtain comparison estimates in subsequent sections. In this regard, a main difficulty arises from the appearance of a nonlocal tail term. More precisely, the tail term interacts with the exit-time (or stopping) cylinders and it also requires technical cares of the solution near the boundary. To deal with this, we further split the tail term appropriately into finitely many quantities over annuli-type cylinders of size comparable to the exit-time radius which contain information on the gradients of the solution and the exterior data.
It is worth mentioning that our main results complement and extend the global almost Lipschitz regularity obtained in
[
25
]
to the case of less regular domains and more general nonlinearities, namely, we assume the domain to satisfy Reifenberg flatness condition and the nonlinearity to have a small BMO seminorm (see Remark
1.8
for details).

1.3
.
Assumptions and Main Results

In this subsection, we present our main problems and corresponding results. We further distinguish into the case of interior estimates for local weak solutions and the case of boundary estimates for the weak solution to Dirichlet problems.
Throughout the article, for
p,q
satisfying (
1.2
) and (
1.3
), we set

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}(\Omega):=\begin{cases}W^{1,p}(\Omega)\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\,\mbox{if }q\leq p,\\
W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)\quad\mbox{if }q>p.\end{cases}

(1.5)

We say that
u\in\mathbb{X}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\Omega)
, if
u\in\mathbb{X}(\tilde{\Omega})
for all
\tilde{\Omega}\Subset\Omega
.
Next, we also set

\displaystyle\texttt{data}:=\{n,s,p,q,\Lambda\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{M}(v;U):=\begin{cases}\|v\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}\quad\,\,\mbox{ if }q>p,\\
1\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\mbox{ if }q\leq p,\end{cases}

(1.6)

where
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
v\in\mathbb{X}(U)
.

1.3.1
.
Interior case

We consider the following general problem in this subsection:

\displaystyle-\textnormal{div}(A(x,Du))+\mathcal{L}u=-\textnormal{div}(|F|^{p-2}F)\quad\text{in}\quad\Omega,

(1.7)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}u(x):=P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))K(x,y)\,dy.

(1.8)

Here, the Carathéodory vector field
A(\cdot,\cdot):\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\})
-regular in the second variable and satisfies the following condition:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}|A(x,z)||z|+|\partial_{z}A(x,z)||z|^{2}\leq\Lambda|z|^{p}\\
\Lambda^{-1}|z|^{p-2}|\xi|^{2}\leq\left<\partial_{z}A(x,z)\xi,\xi\right>\end{cases}

(1.9)

for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and all
z,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and the singular kernel
K(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\leq K(x,y)|x-y|^{n+sq}\leq\Lambda\quad\mbox{for all }x\neq y\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(1.10)

with a constant
\Lambda\geq 1
.
We can assume that
K(x,y)
is symmetric in the sense that
K(x,y)=K(y,x)
, since otherwise we can replace
K(x,y)
with
K^{*}(x,y)=\frac{K(x,y)+K(y,x)}{2}
.

For given
|F|\in L^{p}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\Omega)
, a function
u\in W^{1,p}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\Omega)\cap L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is said to be a local weak solution of (
1.7
) if

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega}A(x,Du)\cdot D\varphi\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))K(x,y)\,dx\,dy\\
&\quad=\int_{\Omega}|F|^{p-2}F\cdot D\varphi\,dx\end{split}

holds for all
\varphi\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}(\Omega)
with compact support contained in
\Omega
. Here, the fractional Sobolev space
W^{s,q}(\Omega)
and the tail space
L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
will be defined later in Section 2.

We next introduce the weak small BMO assumption on the vector field
A
. For this, we write the
n
-dimensional cylinder as
Q_{r}(y)=(y_{1}-r,y_{1}+r)\times B^{\prime}_{r}(y^{\prime})
, where
y\in(y_{1},y^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
B^{\prime}_{r}(y^{\prime})
is the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
with center
y^{\prime}
and radius
r>0
(see Section 2 for precise definitions).

Definition 1.1
.

For given parameters
\delta\in(0,1)
and
R>0
, we say that
A
is
(\delta,R)
-vanishing of codimension 1
if

\displaystyle\sup_{0<r\leq R}\sup_{y\in\Omega}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}(y)}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}(y)}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}(y)}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}(y)}}\kappa(A,Q_{r}(y))(x)\,dx\leq\delta,

(1.11)

where the function
\kappa(A,Q_{r}(y))(\cdot):Q_{r}(y)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as

\displaystyle\kappa(A,Q_{r}(y))(x)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}}\dfrac{|A(x_{1},x^{\prime},z)-\bar{A}_{B^{\prime}_{r}(y^{\prime})}(x_{1},z)|}{|z|^{p-1}}

(1.12)

with
\bar{A}_{B^{\prime}_{r}(y^{\prime})}(\cdot,\cdot):(y_{1}-r,y_{1}+r)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
given by

\displaystyle\bar{A}_{B^{\prime}_{r}(y^{\prime})}(x_{1},z)=\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptB^{\prime}_{r}(y^{\prime})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{B^{\prime}_{r}(y^{\prime})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{B^{\prime}_{r}(y^{\prime})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{B^{\prime}_{r}(y^{\prime})}}A(x_{1},x^{\prime},z)\,dx^{\prime}.

Remark 1.2
.

With the aid of (
1.9
) and (
1.12
), we notice that

\displaystyle|\kappa(A,Q_{r}(y))(x)|\leq 2\Lambda\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in Q_{r}(y)

(1.13)

holds without assuming any vanishing condition on
A
. Moreover, a typical example for
A(x,z)
satisfying (
1.11
) is the following:

\displaystyle A(x,z)=a_{1}(x_{1})a_{2}(x^{\prime})|z|^{p-2}z,

where
a_{1}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
a_{2}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\Lambda^{-1}\leq a_{i}(\cdot)\leq\Lambda
for
i=1,2
, and
[a_{2}]_{\text{BMO}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\leq\delta/\sqrt{\Lambda}
.

Having
\mathbb{X}
given by (
1.5
), and
data
and
\mathcal{M}
as in (
1.6
) in mind, we now state the main theorem in the interior case.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{X}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\Omega)\cap L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a local weak solution of (
1.7
) under the assumptions (
1.2
), (
1.3
), (
1.9
) and (
1.10
). Suppose that
|F|\in L^{\gamma}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\Omega)
for
\gamma>p
. Then, for every
\widetilde{\Omega}\Subset\Omega
, there exist constants
\delta
and
r_{*}\in(0,1)
both depending only on
data
,
\mathcal{M}(u;\widetilde{\Omega})
and
\gamma
such that if
A
is
(\delta,r_{*})
-vanishing of codimension 1, then we have
|Du|\in L^{\gamma}(\widetilde{\Omega})
. Moreover, there exists a constant
c=c(\texttt{data},\mathcal{M}(u;\widetilde{\Omega}),\gamma)
such that

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{R}(x_{0})}}|Du|^{\gamma}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{2R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{2R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{2R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{2R}(x_{0})}}|Du|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+c\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{2R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{2R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{2R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{2R}(x_{0})}}(|F|^{\gamma}+1)\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\quad+c\left(R^{1-\varrho}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus Q_{2R}(x_{0})}\dfrac{|u(y)-(u)_{2R}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}

holds for all
\varrho\in(0,m-qs)
and
Q_{2R}(x_{0})\subset\widetilde{\Omega}
, where
m:=\min\{p,q\}
and
R\in(0,r_{*}/2)
.

Note that Lemma
2.3
,
\mathbb{X}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{s,q}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\Omega)
holds for
s,p
and
q
satisfying (
1.2
) and (
1.3
).

1.3.2
.
Boundary case

With
|F|\in L^{p}(\Omega)
and
g\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}(\Omega^{\prime})\cap L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some bounded open sets
\Omega\Subset\Omega^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we next consider the following Dirichlet problem:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llr}-\textnormal{div}(A(x,Du))+&\mathcal{L}u=-\textnormal{div}(|F|^{p-2}F)\quad\text{in}\quad\Omega,\\
&\ \ u=g\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{in}\quad\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Omega,\end{array}\right.

(1.14)

where the Carathéodory vector field
A(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfies (
1.9
) and
\mathcal{L}
is defined in (
1.8
) with the singular kernel
K(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfying (
1.10
). For fixed
\Omega
and
\Omega^{\prime}
as before, we define the function space

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{X}_{g}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})\\
&:=\left\{v:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:v\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}(\Omega^{\prime})\cap L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),v-g\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega),v=g\,\,\text{a.e. in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Omega\right\}.\end{split}

We also write
\mathbb{X}_{0}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
as the set
\mathbb{X}_{g}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
with
g\equiv 0
. Note that
\mathbb{X}_{{g}}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
is non-empty since
g\in\mathbb{X}_{{g}}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
.

We say that
u\in\mathbb{X}_{g}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
is a weak solution of (
1.14
), if the following holds

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega}A(x,Du)\cdot D\varphi\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))K(x,y)\,dx\,dy\\
&\quad=\int_{\Omega}|F|^{p-2}F\cdot D\varphi\,dx\end{split}

for all
\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{0}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
.

Then we state Reifenberg flatness condition on the domain as below.

Definition 1.4
.

Let two parameters
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2^{n+1}})
and
R>0
be given. A bounded open set
\Omega
is said to be
(\delta,R)
-Reifenberg flat if for each
x_{0}\in\partial\Omega
and
r\in(0,R]
, there is a new coordinate system
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
(which may depend on
r
and
x_{0}
) such that
x_{0}=0
in this new coordinate system and we have

\displaystyle Q_{r}(0)\cap\{x_{1}>\delta r\}\subset\Omega\cap Q_{r}(0)\subset Q_{r}(0)\cap\{x_{1}>-\delta r\}.

Employing the above definition on
\Omega
and Definition
1.1
on the nonlinearity
A
, we introduce the following optimal smallness assumption on
A
and
\Omega
.

Definition 1.5
.

For given parameters
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2^{n+1}})
and
R>0
, we say that
(A,\Omega)
is
(\delta,R)
-vanishing of codimension 1
if for any point
y\in\Omega
and
r\in(0,R]
with

\displaystyle\operatorname{dist}(y,\partial\Omega)=\min_{x_{0}\in\partial\Omega}\operatorname{dist}(y,x_{0})>\sqrt{2}r,

there exists a coordinate system depending on
y
and
r
, whose variables are still denoted by
x=(x_{1},x^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, so that in this coordinate system
y
is the origin and there holds

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}(y)}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}(y)}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}(y)}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}(y)}}\kappa(A,Q_{r}(y))(x)\,dx\leq\delta.

On the other hand, for any point
y\in\Omega
and
r\in(0,R]
with

\displaystyle\operatorname{dist}(y,\partial\Omega)=\operatorname{dist}(y,x_{0})\leq\sqrt{2}r

for some
x_{0}\in\partial\Omega
, there exists a new coordinate system depending on
y
and
r
, whose variables are still denoted by
x=(x_{1},x^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, such that in this coordinate system
x_{0}
is the origin,

\displaystyle Q_{r}(0)\cap\{x_{1}>\delta r\}\subset\Omega\cap Q_{r}(0)\subset Q_{r}(0)\cap\{x_{1}>-\delta r\}

and

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}(x_{0})}}\kappa(A,Q_{r}(x_{0}))(x)\,dx\leq\delta

hold.

Then in conjunction with
\mathbb{X}
,
data
and
\mathcal{M}
given by (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), we are ready to state the main theorem in the boundary case.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{X}(\Omega)\cap\mathbb{X}_{g}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
be a weak solution to problem (
1.14
) under the assumptions (
1.2
), (
1.3
), (
1.9
), (
1.10
), and
g\in\mathbb{X}(\Omega^{\prime})
. Assume that for
\gamma>p
,
|F|\in L^{\gamma}(\Omega)
and
|Dg|\in L^{\gamma}(\Omega^{\prime})
. Then there exist constants
\delta
and
r_{*}
both depending only on
data
,
\mathcal{M}(u;\Omega)
,
\mathcal{M}(g;\Omega^{\prime})
and
\gamma
such that if
(A,\Omega)
is
(\delta,r_{*})
-vanishing of codimension 1, then we have
|Du|\in L^{\gamma}(\Omega_{R/2}(x_{0}))
, for any
x_{0}\in\overline{\Omega}
and
R\in(0,r_{*}/2)
satisfying
Q_{R}(x_{0})\Subset\Omega^{\prime}
. Moreover, there exists a constant
c=c(\texttt{data},\mathcal{M}(u;\Omega),\mathcal{M}(g;\Omega^{\prime}),\gamma)
such that

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\frac{R}{2}}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\frac{R}{2}}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\frac{R}{2}}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\frac{R}{2}}(x_{0})}}|Du|^{\gamma}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{R}(x_{0})}}|Du|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+c\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{R}(x_{0})}}(|F|^{\gamma}+1)\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}

\displaystyle+c\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{R}(x_{0})}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{R}(x_{0})}}|Dg|^{\gamma}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}+c\left(R^{1-\varrho}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus Q_{R}(x_{0})}\dfrac{|u(y)-(u)_{R}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}

holds for all
\varrho\in(0,m-qs)
with
m:=\min\{p,q\}
.

by Proposition
3.4
below, if
\gamma
is close to
p
, then we do not need to assume any vanishing condition on the vector field
A
.

Remark 1.7
.

We remark that our preceding main theorem in the boundary case remains valid under the local assumption on the exterior data
g
with respect to the domain
\Omega^{\prime}
, namely
g\in\mathbb{X}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\Omega^{\prime})
and
|Dg|\in L^{\gamma}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\Omega^{\prime})
, for
\gamma>p
. Since we can find
\Omega\Subset\Omega^{\prime\prime}\Subset\Omega^{\prime}
, so that the aforementioned assumptions are valid globally in
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
, we choose to label
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
as
\Omega^{\prime}
itself to avoid introducing several domains.

Remark 1.8
.

We point out that our global gradient estimate of Theorem
1.6
together with Theorem
1.3
complements the global almost Lipschitz regularity of
[
25
, Theorem 4]
in the case of less regular domains and more general nonlinearities. For instance, let us assume that
g\in W_{{\operatorname{loc}}}^{1,\infty}(\Omega^{\prime})
, for some
\Omega^{\prime}\Supset\Omega
with
\Omega
being a bounded Lipschitz domain. Then for given
f\in L^{n}(\Omega)
, let
u\in\mathbb{X}(\Omega)\cap\mathbb{X}_{g}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
be the weak solution to the exterior data problem (
1.14
) with right-hand side
f
in place of
-{\rm div}(|F|^{p-2}F)
. By the standard regularity (and existence) theory, there exists
F:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying
-{\rm div}(|F|^{p-2}F)=f
in
\Omega
and
|F|\in L^{\gamma}(\Omega)
for any
\gamma>1
. Thus from Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.6
,
|Du|\in L^{\gamma}(\Omega)
for each
\gamma>p
provided
(A,\Omega)
is
(\delta,R)
-vanishing of codimension 1 with sufficiently small
\delta\in(0,1)
and
R>0
depending on
\gamma
. Moreover, by
[
25
, Proposition 2.1]
, we have
u\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Consequently, we get that
u\in C^{\alpha}(\Omega)
for each
\alpha<1
by the Sobolev embedding theorem, provided
\delta
and
R
are sufficiently small depending on
\alpha
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we provide some additional basic notations, function spaces and preliminaries which will be used in the subsequent part of the paper. We also prove the existence result in this section. In Section 3, we prove a small higher integrability result for the gradient of weak solutions. Section 4 is devoted to showing the local estimates on the gradient which is Theorem
1.3
. Finally, in Section 5, we prove the boundary gradient estimate Theorem
1.6
.

2.
Preliminaries

The notation
B_{r}(x_{0})
means the open ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
centered at
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with radius
r>0
. We sometimes abbreviate
B_{r}(x_{0})
with
B_{r}
when its center is not important in the context. Furthermore, for a point
y\in(y_{1},y^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and a radius
r>0
, we write

•

B^{\prime}_{r}(y^{\prime})=\{x^{\prime}=(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}:|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|<r\}
.

•

Q_{r}(y)=(y_{1}-r,y_{1}+r)\times B^{\prime}_{r}(y^{\prime})
.

•

Q^{+}_{r}(0)=Q_{r}(0)\cap\{x:x_{1}>0\}
,
Q^{+}_{r}(y)=Q^{+}_{r}(0)+y
.

•

\Omega_{r}(y)=Q_{r}(y)\cap\Omega
,
\partial_{w}\Omega_{r}(y)=\partial\Omega\cap Q_{r}(y)
.

•

T_{r}(0)=Q_{r}(0)\cap\{x:x_{1}=0\}
,
T_{r}(y)=T_{r}(0)+y
.

We omit the center if it is clear from the context.

Generic positive constants are denoted as
c
and their value may differ from line to line. We often write the dependence of
c
in the parenthesis as
c=c(\texttt{data})
. Sometimes to mention additional dependencies of the constant, we may omit the dependency on
data
.
For
X,Y\in\mathbb{R}
, we say
X\lesssim Y
, if there exists a positive constant
c
such that
X\leq cY
. Moreover, by
X\simeq Y
, we mean that
X\lesssim Y
and
Y\lesssim X
hold. For a measurable function
v
and a measurable set
S
with finite measure, the integral average over
S
is denoted by

\displaystyle(v)_{S}=\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptS}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{S}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{S}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{S}}v\,dx=\dfrac{1}{|S|}\int_{S}v\,dx.

In particular, if
S=Q_{r}
for some
Q_{r}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then we simply denote
(v)_{r}=(v)_{Q_{r}}
.

For
0<s<1\leq p<\infty
and an open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the fractional Sobolev space
W^{s,p}(U)
is defined as below:

\displaystyle W^{s,p}(U):=\{v:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\ :\ \|v\|_{W^{s,p}(U)}<\infty\}

equipped with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{W^{s,p}(U)}
, where

\displaystyle\|v\|_{W^{s,p}(U)}=\|v\|_{L^{p}(U)}+[v]_{W^{s,p}(U)}:=\left(\int_{U}|v|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\left(\int_{U}\int_{U}\dfrac{|v(x)-v(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{n+sp}}\,dxdy\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

We denote the closure of
C^{\infty}_{0}(U)
in
W^{s,p}(U)
as
W^{s,p}_{0}(U)
. Also,
v\in W^{s,p}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(U)
means that
v\in W^{s,p}(\tilde{U})
for all
\tilde{U}\Subset U
. On the other hand, for
0<\ell,\alpha<\infty
, we define the tail space as

\displaystyle L^{\ell}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\bigg\{u:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}\text{ is a measurable function }:\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(y)|^{\ell}}{(1+|y|)^{n+\alpha}}\,dy<\infty\bigg\}.

For
\ell\in[1,\infty)
, we denote the critical Sobolev exponent
\ell^{*}
by

\displaystyle\ell^{*}:=\begin{cases}\dfrac{\ell n}{n-\ell}&\text{if}\,\,\ell<n\\
\text{any number in }(\ell,\infty)&\text{if}\,\,\ell\geq n.\end{cases}

We also define the constant
\theta\in(s,1)
as

\displaystyle\theta:=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\,\,q\leq p,\\
\frac{p}{q}&\text{if}\,\,p<q<\frac{p}{s}\end{cases}

(2.1)

and fix
\tau\in(0,1)
satisfying (thanks to (
1.3
))

\displaystyle\max\left\{s,\dfrac{q-1}{q}\theta\right\}<\tau<\theta.

(2.2)

Now we record some useful embedding results as below.

Lemma 2.1

(Sobolev-Poincaré inequalilty)
.

Let
1<\ell<\infty
and
r>0
. Then, for any
v\in W^{1,\ell}(Q_{r})
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}\left|\dfrac{v(x)-(v)_{r}}{r}\right|^{\ell^{*}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\ell^{*}}}\leq c\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}|Dv|^{\ell}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\ell}},

where
c=c(n,\ell)
is a constant.

Corollary 2.2
.

Assume that
0<s<1<p,q<\infty
and
p>sq
hold.
Let
w\in\mathbb{X}(Q_{r})
, where the space
\mathbb{X}
is as defined in (
1.5
) and
r>0
.
Then for every
\frac{n}{n+p}\leq\sigma\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}|w(x)-(w)_{r}|^{q}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq c\,r^{\theta}\|w\|^{1-\theta}_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r})}\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}|Dw|^{p\sigma}\,dx\right)^{\frac{\theta}{p\sigma}}

for some
c=c(n,p,q)
.

Proof.

We first observe by the Sobolev-Poincaré inequality that for all
\frac{n}{n+p}\leq\sigma\leq 1
, there holds

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}|w(x)-(w)_{r}|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq c\,r\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}|Dw|^{p\sigma}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p\sigma}}.

(2.3)

For the case,
q\leq p
, the required result of the Corollary follows by (
2.3
) and Hölder’s inequality. Moreover, for the case
q>p
, noting that
w\in L^{\infty}(Q_{r})
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}|w(x)-(w)_{r}|^{q}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq c\,\|w\|^{1-p/q}_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r})}\left(\Big(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}|w(x)-(w)_{r}|^{p}\,dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)^{\frac{p}{q}},

which yields the result of the Corollary by using (
2.3
) for
\theta=p/q
.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

Assume that the numbers
0<s<1<p,q<\infty
are such that
p>sq
.
Let
w\in\mathbb{X}(Q_{r})
for
r>0
,
then
w\in W^{s,q}(Q_{r})
and the following holds

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}\int_{Q_{r}}\dfrac{|w(x)-w(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}\,dx\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq c\,r^{\theta-s}\|w\|^{1-\theta}_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r})}\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}|Dw|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{\theta}{p}}

for
c=c(n,s,p,q)
.

Proof.

We first consider the case
r=1
. From
[
29
, Proposition 2.2]
, for
0<t<1\leq\ell<\infty
we have

\displaystyle\|v\|_{W^{t,\ell}(Q_{1})}\leq c\|v\|_{W^{1,\ell}(Q_{1})},

where
c=c(n,\ell,t)
. Set
v(x)=w(x)-(w)_{Q_{1}}
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{1}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{1}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{1}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{1}}}\int_{Q_{1}}\frac{|w(x)-w(y)|^{\ell}}{|x-y|^{n+\ell t}}\,dx\,dy\leq c\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{1}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{1}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{1}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{1}}}|w(x)-(w)_{Q_{1}}|^{\ell}\,dx+c\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{1}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{1}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{1}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{1}}}|Dw(x)|^{\ell}\,dx.

After rescaling and using the Poincaré inequality, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}\int_{Q_{r}}\frac{|w(x)-w(y)|^{\ell}}{|x-y|^{n+\ell t}}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq cr^{-\ell t}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}|w(x)-(w)_{Q_{r}}|^{\ell}\,dx+cr^{\ell-\ell t}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}|Dw(x)|^{\ell}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq cr^{\ell-\ell t}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}|Dw(x)|^{\ell}\,dx.

(2.4)

In the case of
q\leq p
, taking
(\ell,t)=(q,s)
, using Hölder’s inequality and recalling the definition of
\theta
yield the result of the lemma. In the case of
q>p
, since
w\in L^{\infty}(Q_{r})
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}\int_{Q_{r}}\frac{|w(x)-w(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+qs}}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq c\|w\|^{q-p}_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r})}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}\int_{Q_{r}}\frac{|w(x)-w(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{n+p\frac{sq}{p}}}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq cr^{p-sq}\|w\|^{q-p}_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r})}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{r}}}|Dw(x)|^{p}\,dx,

(2.5)

where on the last line we used (
2
) for the choice
(\ell,t)=(p,sq/p)
. Combining (
2
) and (
2
), and again recalling (
2.1
), we conclude the proof.
∎

Now we prove the following existence result.

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose that (
1.2
), (
1.9
) and (
1.10
) hold. Let
\Omega\Subset\Omega^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two bounded domains and let
g\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}(\Omega^{\prime})\cap L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. If
|F|\in L^{p}(\Omega)
, then there exists a unique solution
u\in\mathbb{X}_{g}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
of the problem (
1.14
).

Proof.

We define the functional
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{X}_{0}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})\to\big(W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}(\Omega^{\prime})\big)^{*}
by

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{A}(v),\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}A(x,Dv)\cdot D\phi\,dx

\displaystyle\quad+\left[\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}[v(x)+g(x)-v(y)-g(y)]^{q-1}\big(\phi(x)-\phi(y)\big)K(x,y)\,dx\,dy\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad\quad+2\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Omega^{\prime}}[v(x)+g(x)-v(y)]^{q-1}\phi(x)K(x,y)\,dx\,dy\right]

\displaystyle=:\langle\mathcal{A}_{p}(v),\phi\rangle+\langle\mathcal{A}_{q}(v),\phi\rangle,

for all
\phi\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}(\Omega^{\prime})
, where we have set
[\xi]^{\ell-1}=|\xi|^{\ell-2}\xi
, for
\ell>1
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Note that the operator
\mathcal{A}_{q}
resembles the operator
\mathcal{A}_{0}
defined in
[
8
, Proposition 2.12, page 802]
. Consequently, it can be easily verified that the operator
\mathcal{A}
is monotone, coercive and weakly continuous in
\mathbb{X}_{0}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
; that is,

(i)

for all
v,w\in\mathbb{X}_{0}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
, there holds

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{A}(v)-\mathcal{A}(w),v-w\rangle\geq 0;

(ii)

the following holds

\displaystyle\lim_{\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}(\Omega^{\prime})}\to\infty}\frac{\langle\mathcal{A}(u),u\rangle}{\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}(\Omega^{\prime})}}=\infty;

(iii)

if
\{u_{k}\}\subset\mathbb{X}_{0}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
is such that
u_{k}\to u
in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}(\Omega^{\prime})
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\langle\mathcal{A}(u_{k})-\mathcal{A}(u),\phi\rangle=0\quad\mbox{for all }\phi\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}(\Omega^{\prime}).

Moreover, the map
T_{F}:W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}(\Omega^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
T_{F}(\varphi)=\int_{\Omega}|F|^{p-2}F\cdot D\varphi
is in the dual of
W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q}(\Omega^{\prime})
.
Hence, the existence and uniqueness of
u
follow by the standard theory of monotone operators.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

From now on, we implicitly use the following measure density property.

\displaystyle\text{If }\,\,\Omega\,\,\text{ is }\,(\delta,4R_{0})\text{--Reifenberg flat with }\,\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}\right)\,\text{ and }\,R_{0}>0,

then there holds:

\displaystyle\sup_{0<r\leq 4R_{0}}\sup_{y\in\Omega}\dfrac{|Q_{r}(y)|}{|\Omega\cap Q_{r}(y)|}\leq 4^{n}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\inf_{0<r\leq 4R_{0}}\inf_{y\in\partial\Omega}\dfrac{|Q_{r}(y)\cap\Omega^{c}|}{|Q_{r}(y)|}\geq 4^{-n}.

(2.6)

Now we can show Sobolev-Poincaré inequality on the boundary of the domain with the assumption (
2.6
).

Lemma 2.6

(See
[
3
]
)
.

Let
\Omega
be an open set satisfying (
2.6
) with
R_{0}>0
. Let
Q_{r}\equiv Q_{r}(x_{0})
with
x_{0}\in\Omega
be such that
Q_{\frac{3}{2}r}(x_{0})\not\subset\Omega
, for
r\in(0,R_{0})
. Then, for any
v\in W^{1,\ell}(\Omega_{2r})
with
v=0
on
\partial_{w}\Omega_{2r}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{2r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{2r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{2r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{2r}}}\left|\dfrac{v(x)}{r}\right|^{\ell^{*}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\ell^{*}}}\leq c\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{2r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{2r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{2r}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{2r}}}|Dv|^{\ell}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\ell}},

where
c=c(n,\ell)
is a constant.

We end this section with the following lemma.

Lemma 2.7

(See
[
2
,
30
]
)
.

Define the vector field
V(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\displaystyle V(z):=|z|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}z.

Then, for all
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\vartheta\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\left(A(x,z_{1})-A(x,z_{2})\right)\cdot(z_{1}-z_{2})\eqsim|V(z_{1})-V(z_{2})|^{2}\quad\mbox{for a.e. }x\in\Omega,

(2.7)

and

\displaystyle|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{p}\lesssim\vartheta|z_{1}|^{p}+c(\vartheta)|V(z_{1})-V(z_{2})|^{2}.

(2.8)

3.
Self-improving Property

In this section, we prove the higher integrability (for small exponents) of the gradient of weak solutions. We present the proof only for the boundary case since the interior case can be obtained similarly. Throughout this section, (
1.2
), (
1.9
) and (
1.10
) are assumed. We start with the following Caccioppoli type inequality.

Lemma 3.1

(Caccioppoli inequality)
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{X}_{g}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
be the weak solution to problem (
1.14
). Then for any
\rho\in(0,1)
and
x_{0}\in\Omega
such that
Q_{\rho}\equiv Q_{\rho}(x_{0})\Subset\Omega^{\prime}
, there holds

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\frac{\rho}{2}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\frac{\rho}{2}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\frac{\rho}{2}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\frac{\rho}{2}}}}|Du|^{p}\,dx+|\Omega_{\frac{\rho}{2}}|^{-1}[u]^{q}_{W^{s,q}(Q_{\frac{\rho}{2}})}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\rho^{-p}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|u-g|^{p}\,dx+\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|Dg|^{p}\,dx+\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|F|^{p}\,dx+|\Omega_{\rho}|^{-1}[g]^{q}_{W^{s,q}(Q_{\rho})}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\rho^{-sq}\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}|g-(g)_{\rho}|^{q}\,dx+\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|u-g|^{q}\,dx\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|u-g|\,dx\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus Q_{\rho}}\dfrac{|u(y)-(u)_{\rho}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\,dy\right)

(3.1)

with the implicit constant
c=c(\texttt{data})
.

Proof.

We take
\eta\in C^{1}_{0}(Q_{\rho})
such that
\eta\equiv 1
in
Q_{\rho/2}
,
{\rm supp}(\eta)\subset Q_{2\rho/3}
and
0\leq\eta\leq 1
with
|D\eta|\leq\frac{c}{\rho}
in
Q_{2\rho/3}
.
For
w=u-g
and
\ell:=\max\{p,q\}
, taking
\varphi=\eta^{\ell}w\in\mathbb{X}_{0}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
as a test function in the weak formulation of (
1.14
), we obtain

\displaystyle I_{0}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}A(x,Du)\cdot D(u-g)\eta^{\ell}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\ell\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|A(x,Du)|\eta^{\ell-1}|u-g||D\eta|\,dx+\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|F|^{p-1}|D\varphi|\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{Q_{\rho}}\int_{Q_{\rho}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))K(x,y)\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\quad+2\Lambda\int_{Q_{\rho}}\int_{Q^{c}_{\rho}}\dfrac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-1}|\varphi(x)|}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}-I_{3}+I_{4}.

(3.2)

First, by (
1.9
) and Young’s inequality,
I_{0}
is estimated as

\displaystyle I_{0}\geq\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|Du|^{p}\eta^{\ell}\,dx-\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|Du|^{p-1}|Dg|\eta^{\ell}\,dx\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|Du|^{p}\eta^{\ell}\,dx-c\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|Dg|^{p}\eta^{\ell}\,dx.

For
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
, using the structure condition (
1.9
), the bound on
|D\eta|
and Young’s inequality, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
we have

\displaystyle I_{1}\leq\varepsilon\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|Du|^{p}\eta^{\ell}\,dx+c(\varepsilon)\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|D\eta|^{p}|u-g|^{p}\,dx\leq\varepsilon\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|Du|^{p}\eta^{\ell}\,dx+\dfrac{c(\varepsilon)}{\rho^{p}}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|u-g|^{p}\,dx

and

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|F|^{p-1}|D(u-g)|\eta^{\ell}\,dx+c\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|F|^{p-1}\eta^{\ell-1}|D\eta||u-g|\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|D(u-g)|^{p}\eta^{\ell}\,dx+c(\varepsilon)\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|F|^{p}\eta^{\ell}\,dx+\dfrac{c}{\rho^{p}}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|u-g|^{p}\,dx.

We next write

\displaystyle-I_{3}

\displaystyle=-\underbrace{\int_{Q_{\rho}}\int_{Q_{\rho}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\eta^{\ell}(x)\bar{u}(x)+\eta^{\ell}(y)\bar{u}(y))K(x,y)\,dx\,dy}_{=:I_{31}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\int_{Q_{\rho}}\int_{Q_{\rho}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\eta^{\ell}(x)\bar{g}(x)-\eta^{\ell}(y)\bar{g}(y))K(x,y)\,dx\,dy}_{=:I_{32}},

where we have set
\bar{u}:=u-(u)_{\rho}
and
\bar{g}:=g-(u)_{\rho}
. To estimate the term
I_{31}
, we observe that

\displaystyle|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}

\displaystyle(u(x)-u(y))(\eta^{\ell}(x)\bar{u}(x)-\eta^{\ell}(y)\bar{u}(y))

\displaystyle\geq|\bar{u}(x)-\bar{u}(y)|^{q}\frac{\eta^{\ell}(x)+\eta^{\ell}(y)}{2}-|\bar{u}(x)-\bar{u}(y)|^{q-1}\frac{|\bar{u}(x)+\bar{u}(y)|}{2}|\eta^{\ell}(x)-\eta^{\ell}(y)|.

Now, using the estimate (note that
\ell\geq q
)

\displaystyle|\eta^{\ell}(x)-\eta^{\ell}(y)|\leq q\big(\eta^{\ell}(x)+\eta^{\ell}(y)\big)^{(q-1)/q}|\eta^{\frac{\ell}{q}}(x)-\eta^{\frac{\ell}{q}}(y)|

together with Young’s inequality, we conclude that

\displaystyle|\bar{u}(x)-\bar{u}(y)|^{q-1}\frac{|\bar{u}(x)+\bar{u}(y)|}{2}|\eta^{\ell}(x)-\eta^{\ell}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8}|\bar{u}(x)-\bar{u}(y)|^{q}\frac{\eta^{\ell}(x)+\eta^{\ell}(y)}{2}

\displaystyle\quad+c(|\bar{u}(x)|+|\bar{u}(y)|)^{q}|\eta^{\frac{\ell}{q}}(x)-\eta^{\frac{\ell}{q}}(y)|^{q}.

Thus,

\displaystyle I_{31}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{7}{8}\int_{Q_{\rho}}\int_{Q_{\rho}}|\bar{u}(x)-\bar{u}(y)|^{q}\frac{\eta^{\ell}(x)+\eta^{\ell}(y)}{2}K(x,y)\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\quad-c\int_{Q_{\rho}}\int_{Q_{\rho}}(|\bar{u}(x)|+|\bar{u}(y)|)^{q}|\eta^{\frac{\ell}{q}}(x)-\eta^{\frac{\ell}{q}}(y)|^{q}K(x,y)\,dx\,dy.

(3.3)

On a similar account, we have

\displaystyle|I_{32}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8}\int_{Q_{\rho}}\int_{Q_{\rho}}|\bar{u}(x)-\bar{u}(y)|^{q}\frac{\eta^{\ell}(x)+\eta^{\ell}(y)}{2}K(x,y)\,dxdy+c\int_{Q_{\rho}}\int_{Q_{\rho}}|\bar{g}(x)-\bar{g}(y)|^{q}K(x,y)\,dxdy

\displaystyle\quad+c\int_{Q_{\rho}}\int_{Q_{\rho}}(|\bar{g}(x)|+|\bar{g}(y)|)^{q}|\eta^{\frac{\ell}{q}}(x)-\eta^{\frac{\ell}{q}}(y)|^{q}K(x,y)\,dx\,dy.

(3.4)

Coupling (
3
) and (
3
), and noting
\eta\equiv 1
on
Q_{\rho/2}
, with
c_{*}=c_{*}(\texttt{data})\geq 1
and
c=c(\texttt{data})\geq 1
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}-I_{3}\leq-\dfrac{1}{c_{*}}[u]^{q}_{W^{s,q}(Q_{\rho/2})}+c[g]^{q}_{W^{s,q}(Q_{\rho})}+c\rho^{-sq}\left[\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|u-g|^{q}\,dx+\int_{Q_{\rho}}|u-(u)_{\rho}|^{q}\,dx\right],\end{split}

where we have additionally used the facts
|\eta^{\frac{m}{q}}(x)-\eta^{\frac{m}{q}}(y)|^{q}\leq c\rho^{-q}|x-y|^{q}
in
Q_{\rho}\times Q_{\rho}
and
u-g=0
in
Q_{\rho}\cap\Omega^{c}
.

Next, for
x\in Q_{2\rho/3}
and
y\in Q^{c}_{\rho}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|x-x_{0}|\leq 2\sqrt{2}\rho/3\quad\mbox{ and }\quad|y-x_{0}|\geq\rho,\end{split}

which implies that

\displaystyle|x-y|\geq|y-x_{0}|\left(1-\frac{|x-x_{0}|}{|y-x_{0}|}\right)\geq|y-x_{0}|\left(1-\dfrac{2\sqrt{2}\rho/3}{\rho}\right)=\dfrac{3-2\sqrt{2}}{3}|y-x_{0}|.

(3.5)

On account of the relation
B_{2\rho/3}\subset Q_{2\rho/3}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{Q^{c}_{2\rho/3}}\dfrac{dy}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\leq\int_{B^{c}_{2\rho/3}}\dfrac{dy}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\lesssim\rho^{-sq}.

Therefore, using the above two displays implies that

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{-sq}\int_{Q_{\rho}}|u(x)-(u)_{\rho}|^{q-1}|u(x)-g(x)|\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus Q_{\rho}}\dfrac{|u(y)-(u)_{\rho}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\,dy\int_{Q_{\rho}}|u-g|\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{-sq}\left[\int_{Q_{\rho}}|u-g|^{q}\,dx+\int_{Q_{\rho}}|u-(u)_{\rho}|^{q}\,dx\right]+\left(\int_{Q_{\rho}}|u-g|\,dx\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus Q_{\rho}}\dfrac{|u(y)-(u)_{\rho}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\,dy.

Combining the resulting estimates for
I_{0}
through
I_{4}
with (
3
) and choosing
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
sufficiently small, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\rho/2}}|Du|^{p}\,dx

\displaystyle+[u]^{q}_{W^{s,q}(Q_{\rho/2})}

\displaystyle\leq c\rho^{-p}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|u-g|^{p}\,dx+c\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|Dg|^{p}\,dx+c\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|F|^{p}\,dx+c[g]^{q}_{W^{s,q}(Q_{\rho})}

\displaystyle\quad+c\rho^{-sq}\left(\int_{Q_{\rho}}|u-(u)_{\rho}|^{q}\,dx+\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|u-g|^{q}\,dx\right)

\displaystyle\quad+c\left(\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}|u-g|\,dx\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus Q_{\rho}}\dfrac{|u(y)-(u)_{\rho}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\,dy

(3.6)

with
c=c(\texttt{data})\geq 1
. Finally, applying the following estimate

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}|u-(u)_{\rho}|^{l}\,dx&\leq c(n,l)\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}|g-(g)_{\rho}|^{l}\,dx+c(n,l)\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|u-g|^{l}\,dx\quad(\text{for any }l,\rho>0)\end{split}

(3.7)

in (
3
), we complete the proof of the lemma.
∎

Similar to the above lemma, we can obtain the following estimate which holds inside of the domain
\Omega
. The estimate is proved by testing
\varphi=\eta^{\ell}(u-(u)_{\frac{29\rho}{40}})\in\mathbb{X}_{0}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
to (
1.14
) and applying appropriately modified argument of the proof of Lemma
3.1
.

Corollary 3.2
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{X}_{g}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})
be the weak solution to problem (
1.14
). Then for any
\rho\in(0,1)
and
x_{0}\in\Omega
such that
Q_{3\rho/4}(x_{0})\subset\Omega
, there holds

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{\frac{\rho}{2}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{\rho}{2}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{\rho}{2}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{\rho}{2}}}}|Du|^{p}\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{-p}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}|u-(u)_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}|^{p}\,dx+\rho^{-sq}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}|u-(u)_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}|^{q}\,dx+\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}|F|^{p}\,dx

\displaystyle\quad+\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}}|u-(u)_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}|\,dx\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus Q_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}}\dfrac{|u(y)-(u)_{\frac{29\rho}{40}}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\,dy\Bigg)

(3.8)

with the implicit constant
c=c(\texttt{data})
.

Now we can obtain the following reverse Hölder inequality.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\Omega
be an open set satisfying (
2.6
) with
R_{0}>0
and let
u\in\mathbb{X}_{g}(\Omega;\Omega^{\prime})\cap\mathbb{X}(\Omega)
be a weak solution to problem (
1.14
) with
g\in\mathbb{X}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\Omega^{\prime})
. Assume that
\rho\in(0,\min\{1/8,R_{0}\})
,
x_{0}\in\Omega
and
L\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies
Q_{2^{L+1}\rho}(x_{0})\Subset\Omega^{\prime}
with
2^{L}\rho\leq 1
. Then for each
\nu\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
c=c(\texttt{data},\mathcal{M}(u;\Omega),\mathcal{M}(g;Q_{2^{L}\rho}(x_{0})),\nu)
such that

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\frac{\rho}{2}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\frac{\rho}{2}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\frac{\rho}{2}}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\frac{\rho}{2}}}}|Du|^{p}\,dx+|\Omega_{\frac{\rho}{2}}|^{-1}[u]^{q}_{W^{s,q}(Q_{\frac{\rho}{2}})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq c\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|Du|^{p\sigma}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\sigma}}+c\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|Dg|^{p}\,dx+c\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}(|F|^{p}+1)\,dx

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\nu\left[\sum^{L+1}_{k=1}(2^{k})^{\theta-\frac{q\tau}{q-1}}\left(\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{2^{k}\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{2^{k}\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{2^{k}\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{2^{k}\rho}}}|Du|^{p\sigma}\,dx\right)^{\frac{\theta}{p\sigma}}+\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{2^{k}\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{2^{k}\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{2^{k}\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{2^{k}\rho}}}|Dg|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{\theta}{p}}\right)\right]^{\frac{p}{\theta}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\nu\left(\rho^{1-\varrho}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus Q_{2^{L}\rho}}\dfrac{|u(y)-(u)_{2^{L}\rho}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\,dy\right)^{\frac{p}{m-1}}

(3.9)

holds for all
\sigma\in[\frac{n}{n+p},1]
and
\varrho\in(0,m-qs)
with
m:=\min\{p,q\}
, where
\tau
is as in (
2.2
).

Proof.

To prove the lemma, we will employ the Caccioppoli type inequality and the Sobolev-Poincaré inequality for two different cases:
Q_{\frac{3}{4}\rho}(x_{0})\not\subset\Omega
or
Q_{\frac{3}{4}\rho}(x_{0})\subset\Omega
.

Case 1
: If
Q_{\frac{3}{4}\rho}(x_{0})\not\subset\Omega
holds, then we estimate the right-hand side quantity of (
3.1
) as below, in order to use Lemma
3.1
.
Using Corollary
2.2
and Lemma
2.3
, we have

\displaystyle\rho^{-sq}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}|g-(g)_{\rho}|^{q}\,dx+\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}\int_{Q_{\rho}}\frac{|g(x)-g(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}\,dx\,dy\lesssim\rho^{\theta q-sq}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{\rho})}^{q(1-\theta)}\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773ptQ_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{Q_{\rho}}}|Dg|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{\theta q}{p}}.

(3.10)

On a similar account, using Lemma
2.6
, for all
\frac{n}{n+p}\leq\sigma\leq 1
we get

\displaystyle\rho^{-p}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|u-g|^{p}\,dx\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|Du-Dg|^{p\sigma}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\sigma}}\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|Du|^{p\sigma}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\sigma}}+\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|Dg|^{p}\,dx

(3.11)

and

\displaystyle\rho^{-sq}\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|u-g|^{q}\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{q\theta-sq}\|u-g\|^{q(1-\theta)}_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\rho})}\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|Du-Dg|^{p\sigma}\,dx\right)^{\frac{\theta q}{p\sigma}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{q\theta-sq}\left[\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|Du|^{p\sigma}\,dx\right)^{\frac{\theta q}{p\sigma}}+\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|Dg|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{\theta q}{p}}\right].

(3.12)

Next, set

\displaystyle I:=\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{\kern 1.99997pt\vrule width=6.00006pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.49997pt\kern-8.00003pt\intop}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27773pt\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}{\mathop{\kern 1.00006pt\vrule width=5.0pt,height=3.0pt,depth=-2.59996pt\kern-6.00006pt\intop}\nolimits_{\Omega_{\rho}}}|u-g|\,dx\right)\cdot\tilde{I}^{q-1}\quad\mbox{with}\quad\tilde{I}:=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus Q_{\rho}}\dfrac{|u(y)-(u)_{\rho}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{q-1}}.

(3.13)

Using Minkowski type inequality, we deduce that

\displaystyle\tilde{I}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum^{L-1}_{k=0}\left(\int_{Q_{2^{k+1}\rho}\setminus Q_{2^{k}\rho}}\dfrac{|u(y)-(u)_{\rho}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{q-1}}+\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus Q_{2^{L}\rho}}\dfrac{|u(y)-(u)_{\rho}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{q-1}}
